ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u2	Honourable President of the National Assembly, honourable Members and Members of Parliament, distinguished High Council, ladies and gentlemen! A month ago, the National Assembly ratified the accession treaty between Slovenia and the European Union. Today we have yet another important event, the ratification of the North Atlantic Treaty. The integration of our country into the European Union and then among the fundamental foreign policy objectives has been everything since independence. In order to ensure a better, safer and better quality life for Slovenian citizens and citizens in order to accelerate economic growth and to strengthen the international role, we have made every effort to cooperate actively and equally in international organisations and institutions. The aim was also to provide more security for our country, our nation, our citizens and our citizens. This has also given rise to the knowledge that we must contribute to greater security in Europe and the world as well. The decision to join NATO has thus become an essential component of all state and government programmes in the last 11 years and there has been a broad political consensus on it all this time. It was not only a government project, but it was agreed on by the broader relevant political factors. It was confirmed by the National Assembly, and we confirmed it at the referendum last March. A very broad public debate on NATO membership, which has very well highlighted the various benefits of Slovenia's integration into the alliance, and the majority of Slovenian voters' decisions at the referendum have confirmed the correctness of the political decisions to strengthen Slovenia's security by joining the collective defence organisation. The membership of NATO and the European Union, which alone does not have comparable defence-military capabilities, will end the path of Slovenian integration into the two most important organisations and we will be able to open a new chapter in our history. Today, we can say that the realization of fundamental foreign policy objectives, such as membership in the European Union and NATO, is a commitment to our country's ever more active involvement in the wider international space, and thus to a more effective achievement of greater prosperity and security for our citizens. Indeed, ensuring security is at the heart of the successful economic, social and political development of all countries and societies. This will be the best way to achieve our referendum password last year: Home in Europe, safe in NATO. As early as December 1993, in the resolution on the basis of the design of national security, we have clearly stated that our goal is full membership in the NATO Union. The position adopted by the National Assembly in April 1996 states that the Republic of Slovenia wishes to ensure its fundamental security interest in the collective defence provided by its membership in the NATO Alliance. A year later, a joint statement of parliamentary parties was signed in support of Slovenian integration into NATO. The statement was signed by representatives of both the ruling coalition and the opposition, which proves that the NATO integration project is our joint national project. Critical opinions and objections were also welcome, as they helped to clear up and address a number of dilemmas in our integration into the alliance. The Prague Summit in 2002 was a historic event for both NATO and Slovenia. For us as a turning point in our integration into the alliance, and for the Union, then a more decisive step on the way to the new NATO after the end of the cold war. In this largest and most important cycle of enlargement in the history of the Allies, seven countries, including ours, have been invited to join the accession negotiations and have taken a series of measures to improve military capabilities and to strengthen innovations with partners. The invitation of the Alliance to the accession negotiations on 21 November 2002 at the Prague Summit was undoubtedly a recognition and confirmation of our long-standing commitment and determination to achieve the stated strategic goal of membership in the Alliance. The invitation means recognition to our country of progress made in the decade of our statehood and recognition that through the successful implementation of the action plan for membership and participation in NATO's operations Slovenia has been transformed from a security receiver to a security co-creator. With this, Slovenia has started to become part of a large Euro-Atlantic community of countries that share common democratic values and provide their fundamental security interest within the framework of the collective defence system. That is why we also understand NATO membership as a framework and a means that can effectively help us to establish a modern and well-trained Slovenian army through the process of broad and thorough defence-military reforms, as a basis for the security of our country and for contributing to the collective defence of the allies. The professionalization of the army was good news in Slovenia. The possibility and opportunity for military activities to be dealt with by those who want and can do so, while ensuring that top professionals take care of security and defence. I would say that this is one of the key links of our entry into both Euro-Atlantic integration. Just as we did not harmonise ourselves with the European Union because of it, but because of our own development, we are also professionalising the armies not only for compatibility with NATO standards, but for our greater security. The Slovenian professional army will be in the service of the security of all citizens and citizens and will be in the service of the country. The ten-year period of relations between Slovenia and the NATO Union was very dynamic. But it has already begun with our cooperation in the Partnership for Peace. The entry process itself has been carried out in very changed international circumstances, marked by the transformation of global and regional power centres, the processes of globalisation, new forms of security threats, the dynamics of transatlantic relations, the processes of stabilisation in the Balkans. Both Slovenia and the Union were then and are still in the process of reform. During this period, Slovenia has developed and built its armed forces since the creation of a independent and sovereign state with the aim of building a professional Slovenian army, adapting to the standards of alliance and operating under collective defence conditions. Although reforms in the Slovenian Army are primarily in our own interest, this was also a reciprocal and two-way process based on the exchange of knowledge and experience when we joined NATO. The process incorporated into NATO has undoubtedly contributed to the acceleration of our defence-military reforms. This process has been particularly useful in accelerating the formation of a transparent structure of armed forces under democratic and civil control. Even after the fall of the Berlin Wall or the end of the Cold War, after the collapse of the Warsaw Pact and even more after 11 September 2001, NATO found itself in a deep and dynamic process of transforming, changing and responding to the new threats and security challenges posed by the 21st century. During this period, NATO, if I list only some milestones, has strengthened cooperation with non-member countries through the Partnership for Peace programme, in which we have also successfully participated. It responded to human rights violations in south-eastern Europe and intervened in order to establish them. It established a strong partnership with the Russian Federation and Ukraine, expanded with three new members, and for the first time since 11 September 2001 it has activated Article 5 of the Washington Treaty, not least taking over the leadership of international forces to stabilise the situation in Afghanistan. The transformation of the relationship is not yet complete, but the fundamental principles remain unchanged. Today we are entering an association of democratic and developed countries, which in five and a half decades have established the most effective political defence alliance in the world, acting on the basis of the UN Charter. During the decades of the Cold War, he provided conditions for the smooth development of democracy in Europe and consequently established such a level of security that allowed for the deepening and expansion of European integration processes. It is then an alliance of democratic countries that make decisions unanimously. Through the Washington Treaty, it guarantees its members the collective defence, the synergy of security defence efforts and the possibility of optimal exploitation of available resources. It is also the only organisation that provides transatlantic security and defence connectivity and effective dialogue on both sides of the Atlantic, which is necessary to successfully address global problems. The NATO Member States are aware that the security challenges are too complex for them to be part of one more capable organisation. It therefore cooperates with various institutions, organisations and countries in establishing a broad security network. The Alliance strengthens institutional relations with organisations such as the UN, OSCE and the EU and deepens existing relations with partner countries in the Euro-Atlantic area, in the wider Mediterranean and in particular with the Russian Federation. Slovenia is already part of this distributed security network. As a member of the Union, then we will have the opportunity to play an even more active role. A secure and stable environment is a fundamental condition for the smooth economic, social and cultural development of each country, nation and individual. Membership in NATO allows us, together with other members, to decide on security and political issues and to participate actively in calming down crisis hotspots while at the same time sharing the burden in the alliance provides the necessary military and defence security in our country. As an equal part of the international community, we also accept our share of the obligation to ensure international peace, stability and security. Slovenian soldiers in peace support operations carry out all the more demanding tasks. Because of the growing interrelationship and interdependence of the modern world, as well as the indivisibility of world peace and security, action is essential today. Each country must bear its share of the responsibility to maintain world peace and security and cannot be just a consumer of security. Slovenia is ready to take on an active role. We are also prepared to actively cooperate and co-decision in the creation of a new NATO so that together we can respond to the difficult and not always predictable security challenges of the 21st century. We will be fully co-decisiond on this at the upcoming Istanbul summit of NATO. Ladies and gentlemen, you have before you the proposal to ratify the North Atlantic Treaty. The year 2004 is unique and historic for Slovenia. It represents the summit of our multi-annual efforts and efforts and at the same time marks the beginning of our new role in the international community. It is therefore right to reiterate that a secure and stable environment is a prerequisite for smooth economic, social, cultural, scientific, technological and social development. With our NATO membership, we want to ensure a secure environment, an environment that will contribute to the smooth development of our country and its people. The integration into the collective defence system will also make it easier for Slovenia to face contemporary threats to security and stability. Of course, we also want to contribute to the security of all the allies in the organization, just as the allies would contribute to our security. As a full member, both NATO and the European Union, we want to contribute to the expansion of cooperation and partnership between the two organisations and the achievement of common strategic interests, particularly in the Balkans. I believe that we will also succeed because we are fully aware of the key importance of the principle of solidarity in alliance. These experiences will be passed on to all those who are needed and who want to continue our journey, especially to our neighbours in South-Eastern Europe. We want to contribute to enhancing the security of everyone in the Euro-Atlantic area and to achieving the common objective of a comprehensive and free Europe, an association in peace and with common values for the democracy of the market economy, the rule of law and respect for human rights. On this occasion, I would like to thank all of you who have participated in the implementation of this national project. I would also like to thank the Member States of the Alliance and NATO for all the support, encouragement and assistance we have received on this difficult path. As a partner and candidate for membership in these years of intensive cooperation, we have shown that we are worthy of trust and are able to contribute as best we can to strengthening the alliance and peace, security and stability in Europe and in the world. On April 4th this year, NATO will celebrate its 55th anniversary. And then, as a full member, Slovenia will celebrate. This will be the first great victory of the new member and we will do everything we can to make all the future Slovenian victories, the triumphs of just peace, solidarity and peace, this is our commitment to citizens and citizens, this is Slovenia's commitment to the modern world. Thank you.

This brings Slovenia into the area and becomes the centre, the source of security and peace in Europe. This will meet our country's strategic foreign policy ambition. Slovenia has been honoured by the constant, unequivocal and active support of the National Assembly, the clear positions of the Assembly and the intensive engagement of the IZZ, the Government, the President of the Republic and some civil society organisations, such as the NATO Committee and the Atlantic Council. Prior to last year's referendum, they created conditions for convincing confirmation of Slovenian national efforts. Ladies and gentlemen, honourable Members! On 5 February 2004, this said a few days ago, the last member of the North Atlantic Alliance ratified the accession protocols of the seven invited countries. The Washington Treaty is thus open to accession and it is anticipated that on 2 April, invited countries will deposit instruments on the ratification of the Treaty to the depositary, the United States of America, and become members of the North Atlantic Alliance. I suggest you confirm the North Atlantic Treaty. Today, we can be satisfied; remember how much we wanted to enter NATO in 1997 and how disappointed we were when we failed at that time. Let us also remember how hot they want to enter NATO some other countries that have not been heard so far. Today's event is a great achievement of Slovenian national competitiveness, but of course we also have a great responsibility. Slovenia has clearly supported NATO’s policy objectives by actively engaging in NATO operations and, last but not least, by fulfilling the tasks set out in the Membership Action Plan, has demonstrated that it is a reliable and credible partner who meets the required membership criteria and is entitled to membership in the most effective political defence relationship in the world. According to the results of the referendum, NATO was relatively few real opponents in Slovenia, but they were influential and visible. The moment of the referendum coincided with the outbreak of the Iraq war, which some unjustifiably linked to NATO. But these dilemmas and conflicts are now behind us. Slovenian voters strongly supported Slovenia's membership in NATO. I believe that, thanks to the referendum and the activities associated with it, they are well aware of the importance of this membership. When it comes to crucial historical issues, Slovenians are decided on a prudent and state-of-the-art basis. In Slovenia we have experienced the same hardships and internal struggles we have experienced in other European countries. After one year, we find that these fights are more or less over. The style and solidarity return to the Euro-Atlantic community. In the National Assembly and in the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, we believed that it could not be otherwise. By entering NATO, we manifest the belongings of the communities of countries whose development is based on democracy, personal freedom and the rule of law, peace and trust in the purposes and principles of the UN Charter. Slovenia joins the family of countries that have decided to combine their security efforts due to synergies. Membership in NATO strengthens the weight of Slovenia in the international environment and enables us to co-decision in the design of the security policy of the Euro-Atlantic area. Involved in the process of taking security decisions, Slovenia has been given the opportunity to provide quality and timely knowledge of the information and trends relevant to its safety. Of course, it is a two-way process, as the allies expect Slovenia to recognize, in accordance with its capabilities, its proportionate part of the responsibility for the effective and effective functioning of the Atlantic Alliance. Slovenia will strengthen its credibility with a responsible attitude towards NATO membership in a broader international context. Most NATO members are also members of the European Union. This is about the countries with which Slovenia realizes the most of its political, economic and cultural interests. Slovenia is one of the most successful and most respected countries with NATO membership. Then, as we know, he was created in a different situation than today. However, after the fall of the Berlin Wall, he began to adapt successfully to the new security challenges. This is a continuous process, the fundamental objective of which is to ensure an optimum level of security for the members. Globalisation goes beyond national borders, which is particularly true for different forms of security threats. It is therefore understandable that countries do not guarantee their security only at their borders. Responsibility and solidarity also mean concern for stability in neighbouring regions. Their imbalances could have negative consequences for the security situation in the Member States in NATO. Illegal migration, all kinds of illegal trafficking, drugs, people, weapons and so on, organised crime and terrorism are only an external expression of the security situation in regions or countries whose internal instability is spreading across their borders. New Nata, allow me to say a few words about him, is increasingly dealing with new threats and threats, including terrorism. Terrorism occurs with different names, sometimes even those that are at first acceptable, justified or even acceptable. [[...]] [[Incomprehensible.]] Terrorism emerges with excuses of extreme force, of hopeless majority and minority, of fateful power and powerlessness, often fueling feelings and compassion, and always fueling new disputes. [[...]] As it is not possible to accept an interpretation of democracy that would allow permanent majority and permanent minorities, of course, neither can any violent challenge by any majority and no minority. The only way to solve these problems is by taking into account the laws and international law. There is no excuse in the community, including Slovenia, for terrorism. It then increasingly provides a framework for non-military activities carried out by various international, governmental and non-governmental organisations seeking to regulate the situation in crisis areas. So far, NATO's engagement in South-East Europe and Afghanistan, where NATO is carrying out a UN-authorised peace operation, can serve as a good example. The efforts of the international community in Afghanistan, the key part of which is also the presence of NATO, have allowed the return of more than one and a half million refugees who have been living in refugee camps outside their homeland since the Soviet invasion of 1979. The dynamics of modern security requires that NATO pursue a proactive policy that does not only respond to situations where they develop into difficult regional and international crises. This was also recognized by members of NATO, who took a decision at the Prague Summit on the establishment of NATO forces for rapid intervention, the so-called "Nato response forces". Their very existence will be a dissuasive factor for countries and groups that may create imbalances in their region. However, for the effective and timely functioning of the Alliance, it is not only a military structure that can respond quickly to crisis situations but also an appropriate adjustment to the speed of political decision-making, which, given the fact that it is a democratic state, is certainly a difficult task. Military engagement is only one of the dimensions of NATO's pro-active policy, while others form different forms of partnership that enables non-members of NATO to help reform their defence systems while training them to engage in quality international operations of the North Atlantic Alliance. This alliance also encourages regional cooperation of partner countries through the Partnership for Peace, as it primarily contributes to reducing tensions and reducing latent conflict situations in the security regions. Ladies and gentlemen, honourable Members! Slovenia is involved in a long-term security regime in its south-eastern neighbourhood and encourages various forms of cooperation with NATO in this area. Our membership in the Alliance and cooperation with its members will significantly strengthen the possibilities of Slovenian security engagement in the Western Balkans, whose permanent stabilisation is in the vital interest of Slovenia. As is known and understood, this stabilisation also has economic dimensions for us. Slovenia supports the enlargement of NATO with all those countries that want to do so and meet the conditions for membership. The enlargement so far has significantly broadened the area of institutional stability in Europe and thus significantly deplores the security burden of the countries that have recently emerged from the pinnacle of authoritarian systems. Already the NATO enlargement forecast and intensive partnership have successfully included modern European thinking and efforts for closer integration in this part of Europe. The formal entry of Central European countries into NATO means irreversible positive security processes in this part of Europe. One of the key elements of European security and stability is transatlantic security cooperation, which has reached the highest degree of institutionalisation right beyond the existence of NATO. The most effective and, by all means, the only way to deal with the security threats of the present time is to face them together. Dear Members, honourable Members, ladies and gentlemen, at the NATO Summit in Istanbul on 28 and 29 June this year, we will be actively involved as a member in the debate on the future role and importance of the Atlantic Alliance. This will be an opportunity to consider and contribute, together with those who have been more skeptical about membership, to the formation of such a NATO as is in accordance with Slovenian interests and views. NATO's security policy and engagement after the end of the Cold War showed that then, in changed circumstances, it retains its security relevance as part of broader efforts in the international community to establish peace, security, security and balance in the world. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u6	Dear colleagues, presidents, members of the government, diplomatic assembly, distinguished guests and guests, citizens and citizens! The Committee of the National Assembly of the Republic of Slovenia for Foreign Policy considered the proposal for a law on ratification of the North Atlantic Treaty as the parent working body at its 78th regular meeting, held on 17 February 2004. In its opening remarks, the State Secretary at the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, Samuel Žbogar, explained that, in accordance with Article 51 of the Charter and the United Nations, on 4 April 1949, in Washington, 12 States Parties had established the North Atlantic Treaty. I will not repeat the Government's argument because this has been discussed by government representatives. I have presented the content of the work of the Committee on Foreign Policy of the National Assembly, political parties and other branches of government, civil society and, last but not least, citizens and citizens of Slovenia in the process of self-reliance and integration into the NATO Union, which I have identified as the second most important event of this century when it comes to ratifications. I pointed out that in the fight for Slovenian independence and in the face of the consequences of military conflicts in our immediate neighbourhood, we wanted the intervention of the Atlantic Alliance, while at the same time asking ourselves the question of the essence of its existence. The answer to the question whether we are prepared to expose our soldiers, our defences and our lives for our own safety has long been avoided by the Slovenian public. Before the Member States invited us to join the NATO Union, they contributed, with their presence and victims in their midst, to regulating the situation in our immediate neighbourhood. At the meeting of the committee, we bowed silently to the soldiers of the member states of the Atlantic Alliance, who had spent the last decade of their lives trying to stabilise the situation in South-East Europe. In mentioning the important actions of the National Assembly in joining the Union, I stressed in particular the importance of the joint statement of parliamentary parties in support of Slovenia's integration into the NATO Union, signed on 17 April 1997 by all the Presidents of the then parliamentary parties and both Members of the Nationality. The first document, which provided the basis for Slovenia's involvement in the Atlantic Alliance, was a motion for a resolution on the origins of the national security design, which was considered by the International Relations Committee in 1993. In 1994, the Committee discussed the Partnership for Peace initiative. At the same time, the Committee invited the Government to strengthen the representation of the Republic of Slovenia at NATO headquarters in Brussels. The year 1996 was a turning point in many areas. As a first step towards professionalization of the Slovenian Armed Forces, he was aware that with military bonds on the service of military service it was not possible to go on any operations abroad and that we need a professional army set up by the 1st Battalion for international cooperation. It was cooperation with the NATO Alliance in the Partnership for Peace that forced us to embark on the path of professionalization of the Slovenian Army, which has now led to the possibility that we could put an end to the service of the military and come to a fully professional army. In 1996, the first joint Slovenian-Italian military exercise was important in Cyprus or the Gulf of Tržaška, and the first exercise of the Slovenian Armed Forces took place in the fall of the same year, with the participation of Dutch aircraft. "Then the energy facility act" was first mentioned by Slovenia and designated it as a recipient of the US budget for preparation for membership in the NATO Union. Despite a number of events that showed that Slovenia could be invited to the Atlantic Alliance in 1997, no invitation was received. Today we can assess that there were no conditions for enlargement to Slovenia at that time. The integration of Poland, the Czech Republic and Hungary into the Alliance was primarily and firstly the correction of historical injustices. I have stressed that the proposal for a law on the ratification of the North Atlantic Treaty coincides with an extremely difficult and crucial event for the Slovenian Army, with the deployment of our soldiers to Afghanistan in the framework of an ISA operation. Slovenia is the only successor to the former Yugoslavia, which is militarily present in both SFOR and KFOR operations, and has also participated in Alba and Jantar Fox operations and operations in Cyprus. The pre-referendum public opinion polls showed that the proportion identified for NATO was much lower than that found in the referendum. A lot of voter support is the power to attribute a well-founded and non-aggressive pre-referendum campaign. A positive correlation has been established in the Slovenian public between NATO's intervention in the South-Eastern Europe area and the stabilisation of the situation in the area. He then intervened in Bosnia and Herzegovina with the mandate of the UN and the Federal Republic of Yugoslavia without the consent of the UN because of the veto of Russia and then entered the Pristina airport alone on the day before the start of the KFOR operation. The Atlantic Alliance also worked preventively in Macedonia. As a support for the pre-referendum campaign, it is also necessary to mention the visits made by a number of foreign representatives of governments and parliaments to show and explain the benefits of their countries' membership in the NATO Union. Last but not least, the murder of Zoran Djindjic was further reminded by the Slovenian public that the Balkans are still a restless and unpredictable area. Slovenia supports the further enlargement process not only with the successors of the former Yugoslavia but also with Albania and all the countries of South-Eastern Europe. I then marked the entry into the Union as a national project in which both the opposition and all the ruling coalitions have proved themselves. Members stressed in particular the concerted performance of all political actors and in particular the role of political parties in the process of joining the Atlantic Alliance. Only Bevk recalled that the United List of Social Democrats established its position towards joining the NATO Union in 2001 in a resolution at the Koper Party Congress entitled "To accelerate Slovenia's preparations for joining Euro-Atlantic links". At the 2002 conference in Otocec, they reiterated their support for joining the Atlantic Alliance, assuming that the will of citizens and citizens at the referendum should be respected. He announced that a large majority of the members of the United Nations Group of Social Democrats would support the ratification of the North Atlantic Treaty. Aurelio Juri announced that he would abstain in the vote, as the results of the referendum were clear, but could not support the bill because of the incredibility of the treaty which, in its preamble and in Article 1, swears on the authority of the United Nations and on the peaceful settlement of disputes, the principles violated by members of the United States of America and the United Kingdom by an attack on Iraq without the consent of the United Nations. The President of the Defence Committee, Rudolf Petan, recalled the important milestones in Slovenia's accession to the Euro-Atlantic Alliance and stressed the maturity and mutual participation of all actors and, in particular, political parties in the NATO integration process. He noted that the drafter of the joint statement of parliamentary parties in support of Slovenia's integration into the Union was NATO Janez Janša, but that the project of approximation to the Euro-Atlantic Alliance exceeded the party framework and became a national project. During his discussion, Dorijan Marsič praised the Ministry of Defence, in particular the Slovenian Army, which carried out a number of key defence reforms in a relatively short time. The direct result of the entry into NATO is also the professionalization of the Slovenian Army. He stressed that Slovenia should support the further enlargement process in the future, and should support Croatia's membership in the NATO Alliance and its efforts in relation to the membership action plan and Bosnia and Herzegovina and Serbia-Montenegro in the Partnership for Peace. He said that the Atlantic Alliance had not participated in the attack on Iraq and was not directly involved in the post-war reconstruction of Iraq. However, Jožef Jerovšek pointed out that Slovenia does not lose its sovereignty by entering the Union and that it has gained an equal position with its accession to the Alliance than has been the case with Slovenia. In the debate, MEPs Jože Ašič, Ivan Mamićter Jožef Kavticnik pointed out the importance of the NATO Alliance and the protection of human rights as one of the principles of the Atlantic Alliance. They also stressed the need to celebrate the ratification of the Treaty in the National Assembly. Ivan Bozič mentioned in his contribution the role and contribution of the former party of the Slovenian Christian Democrats in joining the Alliance. Mag. Zoran Gračner highlighted five key reasons for supporting Slovenia's integration into the NATO Union, the geostrategic position of our country, the lack of an alternative option in the case of non-involvement, membership in NATO as recognition of Slovenia's achievements after independence, commitment to the human will expressed in the referendum, and professionalization of the Slovenian Army. In addition, the Secretary of State at the Ministry of Defence, Dr. Miran Jazbec, highlighted the role of the Ministry of Defence in the NATO accession project. At the end of the discussion, the Secretary of State at the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, Samuel Žbogar, thanked the Committee on Foreign Policy and the Committee on Defence for all support and constructive cooperation in the process of approaching the NATO Union. To the members of the Committee, I proposed an additional decision: the Committee expressed its expectation that, at the time of the ratification of the North Atlantic Treaty, the large hall, the lobby of the large hall and the building of the National Assembly would be adorned by the flags of the Member States and the future Member States and the NATO Union. Aurelio Yuri objected. Members of Dorijan Maršič, Ivan Bernik, Jožef Jerovšek and Rudolf Petan, pointing out also the practice in other acceding countries, advocated the celebration of the event. In its consideration of the law on ratification of the North Atlantic Treaty, the Committee took into account the opinion of the Legal and Legislative Service, which, in terms of compliance with the Constitution and the legal system and from a legislative and technical point of view, had no comments. After the discussion, the Committee adopted all the articles of the bill with 14 votes in favour and no "against". As no amendment to the draft law has been adopted, the Committee on Foreign Policy proposes to the National Assembly that the proposal be dealt with in the text submitted to the National Assembly for legislative procedure and adopted. The rapporteur at the meeting of the National Assembly will be the chairman of the Committee. The Committee then, with 14 votes in favour and 1 against, and addressed to the President of the National Assembly, the following decision: "The Committee expressed its expectation that, at the time of the ratification of the North Atlantic Treaty, the great hall, the courtyard of the Grand Hall and the building of the National Assembly will be adorned on occasion by the flags of the Member States and future members of the NATO Alliance." Dear colleagues and colleagues, I now suggest that, just as we did on the Foreign Policy Committee, we honour with silence the memory of all those who have put their lives into the pursuit of peace and stability in Southeast Europe. [[One minute of silence.]] Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u7	Thank you, Mr. Chairman of the Committee. I now give my word and invite you to speak for the chairman of the Defence Committee, Comrade Rudolph Petan. Please, Mr. Chairman of the Committee.
ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u8	Mr President, high-ranking guests, colleagues and colleagues! Today's ratification process of the North Atlantic Treaty is the final act of our approach to the Alliance, which began exactly ten years ago. In January 1994, the National Assembly of the Republic of Slovenia adopted a supplement, a new twelfth point to the resolution on the origins of the national security concept, which reads as follows: "In the process of integration into the European and other international economic and political links, the Republic of Slovenia assumes at the same time the rights and responsibilities required by these links in the defence field, except for the provision of peacekeeping forces in the area of the former Yugoslavia until the end of the crisis. This is all the more so because she is vitally interested in establishing an effective collective security system in her neighbourhood, Europe and the world. As an important goal, it places the inclusion in NATO or the Western European Union as a pillar, the European pillar of NATO. The decision on these important links in the defence field must take place on the basis of a broad professional and public exchange of views. On these issues, the National Assembly may hold a referendum." This is what was written in the resolution, which was then written down. Today we are concluding this process, and this is certainly a glorious event. This is a great success for Slovenia, a great event, especially if we are aware that 13 years ago we were part of a Balkan boiler that no such heavy lid could afford to continue. The human will and desire for democratisation were too great to allow such a situation to continue. Slovenia was then on its way. Others did, too. Slovenia, which has always been associated with Central Europe in its history, except for the last 70 years of the past century, has opted for Euro-Atlantic links, for the European Union and for NATO. Therefore, it is no wonder that Slovenia joined the Partnership for Peace in March 1994 and became an associate partner in the North Atlantic Assembly in the same year. For all these steps, including the transformation of the armed forces, the state had to prepare itself; it had to adopt a legal framework, i.e. relevant legislation. There have been problems here, we have sometimes adopted subordinate acts before, and we have made general adaptations to them. In doing so, it must be stressed that we did not build our armed forces, the Slovenian Armed Forces for the sake of others, we did not build them because of our integration into NATO, but mainly because of ourselves. Then it was only a catalyst that we had done certain tasks before, which was of paramount importance for a country in transition. We have further outlined the material bases in the law of providing funds for the implementation of the fundamental development programmes of the defence forces of the Republic of Slovenia. We did this for the first time in 1994 until 2007 and later upgraded until 2010. This project was also accompanied by problems at the beginning of the non-quality supply and the share of gross domestic product fell to 1.22% in 2000. Although we promised differently, it has been difficult for us to prove our credibility in many ways abroad. However, this has changed since 2000. One of the most important steps in our rapprochement to NATO, which at that time was no longer just a defence liaison, is to sign a joint statement on NATO integration. This was done on 17 April 1997, and this project was prepared by Janez Janša. By signing all parliamentary parties, this project was no longer a single option project, nor was it a political party, nor was it a government or opposition project, but it became a national project. This has proved that we can go beyond the party framework and prove our maturity when it comes to important decisions of the Slovenian people. The fact that this is a joint project is also one of the reasons for a favourable result at the March 2003 referendum. And why is NATO so important to us? Because of the values on which the Alliance is based and for which we have taken over since 1990, these are: stable democratic, social and political systems, a functioning market economy, respect for human rights, democratic control of armed forces and regulated relations with neighbours. However, Member States are expected to express their determination and the ability together to defend the freedom, heritage and values of nations and countries that are based on these principles. In the National Assembly, we have adopted several documents for the transformation of the armed forces and for our placement in a wider space. I would mention only some: a long-term programme for the development and equipping of the Slovenian Armed Forces by 2010, a resolution on the national security strategy, a law on the completion of the Slovenian Armed Forces and a law on military duty. This is certainly one of the most important laws, since it means the abolition of the recruitment system, which was then so much before the collapse, and the introduction of a professional army, which means a quality leap and allows us to engage in international peace operations in the long term. Slovenia has always been aware of its responsibility to contribute to peace, particularly in its neighbourhood. Our units have been in Albania, Cyprus, and are still within the framework of SFOR in Bosnia, KFOR in Macedonia and Kosovo, and I am certainly part of our decision to deploy our troops to Afghanistan. If anyone, then Slovenia is the one who can appreciate peace. Slovenia is in the immediate neighbourhood of the Balkans, where wars were a common way to resolve conflicts with armed conflicts. Foreign verification was also very important for the construction of our armed forces and the integration into NATO. In doing so, I would particularly like to mention the work of the Garrett Commission, which has identified the situation with us and made recommendations. I emphasize, recommendations, not demands. A lot of sobriety has made our drawbacks in Nato in 1997 in Sintra and then confirmation at the Madrid Summit. Only Poland, the Czech Republic and Hungary were invited at that time, without Slovenia. For the second round of enlargement, Slovenia, as well as other candidates, had to prepare action plans for inclusion in the membership in which the intentions were defined and how we will specify these, as well as the material conditions. In 2002, the government also identified a projection of defence expenditure by 2010 and, of course, it was also discussed by the Defence Committee. In the same year, the amended Defence Act addressed the issue of State participation in the collective defence system. A new structure of the Slovenian Armed Forces has also been established, information and security activities have been organised for defence purposes, a system of career paths and so on. However, there is still no structural staff mismatch. The most important milestone in our integration, while approaching NATO means a powder summit where we have received such a long-awaited and desirable invitation to join the Alliance. In doing so, we should not forget what has allowed us to be in the process of ratifying the North Atlantic Treaty today. This is our decision on a separate path that was realized in June 1991. ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u22	Dear Mr President, Excellency, high-ranking guests, colleagues and colleagues! When we voted on this resolution in 1993 in this National Assembly, there were many Members against, above all, those who today constitute a ruling political option. Nevertheless, the resolution was adopted. This opened the way to NATO, in early 1994 we signed the Partnership for Peace and thus entered the waiting room for NATO. An important shift was reached in 1997 when all the parliamentary parties signed our proposal for a cooperation agreement to enter NATO, which has already been mentioned, and since then this political unity has endured more or less, with a period of some years since that anti-Nat wave started, which was short-lived. In 1999, we proposed for the first time, in accordance with these guidelines in the Slovenian Democratic Party, data such as were on the table, the professionalization of the Slovenian army, the formation of a professional army and a voluntary reserve, also due to the entry into an alliance that brings new tasks. The government was then against it, it was four years before this proposal was implemented, and it is on the nose with a lot of improvisation and the consequences that we feel today. Today Slovenia is like some other members, the new members of NATO are insufficiently prepared to participate in the alliance, we have not heard about this today, although this is no secret, in the alliance as a whole, but we are not the only ones, this must be acknowledged. We have overcapacity, this is due to improvisation, it took us two years to send some soldiers to Afghanistan, and there are all of them, including many smaller countries. Inadvertently, funds were spent from basic dedicated programmes or development programmes for the Slovenian military, and aircraft were bought for civilian purposes and so on. Every year, the government goes to hideouts with defense expenses. That's why we're incredible with other partners in NATO, future partners in NATO. Never since we've been in the Partnership for Peace in ten years have we announced such defence expenditures as we have actually achieved them are they always different. Unfortunately, in our Slovenian army, we still have strong remnants of the mentality of the former JLA, which is hidden under the concept of profession, but this profession was defeated in the Balkan wars at the end of the previous century. It is more of a backward mentality that is not compatible with NATO standards, but unfortunately it has a dominant influence in the Slovenian army. And, of course, too little is the emphasis on the creation of a voluntary reserve, which, according to our potentials, is still at the heart of the defence power of the Slovenian state. But all the shortcomings I'm talking about aren't the reason against joining NATO, but they're the reasons for getting in as soon as possible. We are pleased that this step is taking place, and we are pleased that the agreement proposed and signed in 1997 has been maintained. We are pleased to join the countries in which citizens share the same values as us, and therefore we have had no difficulty in raising the flag of NATO, the European Union, at the premises of the national assembly, although unfortunately the colleges of this national assembly have had great difficulties. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u26	Dear Members and Members! Dear Mr. President, the esteemed High Council! Eleven years ago, when there was a war near us, the National Assembly, in a resolution on the origins of the national security concept, determined that our vital interest was to establish an effective security of the country by including it in NATO. Ten years ago, we joined the Partnership for Peace programme. Four years ago, we presented NATO with the first programme of implementation of the membership action plan. Two years ago, in Prague, we received an invitation to full membership in the North Atlantic Alliance. Today, we also formally reaffirm the achievement of this strategic objective. These milestones tell us everything. We are pursuing the objective pursued in a purposeful, demanding and responsible manner. Our country's membership in the collective defence system means today that the global security environment is constantly changing, the highest possible security of the country that we can achieve by taking into account our defence capabilities. We thus ensure our security and the security of the members of the Alliance directly and alongside the most influential countries in the world. The basic mission of the military capabilities of the Alliance is clear, to safeguard peace, to ensure territorial integrity, independence and the security of the members. With changes in the international security environment, NATO is also changing. The threats and challenges of the modern world have become complex and global, making the alliance a global factor in ensuring and safeguarding international peace, security and stability. After independence, our country had to establish a defence function and a defence system completely new. Membership in NATO therefore means that we knew and managed to connect the defence system with the defence systems of modern countries. This also allows us to take into account their experiences and proven solutions. This increases the efficiency, rationality and economic use of the material, human and other capacities that we have at our disposal. As a credible and responsible member of the international community, we assume our share of responsibility for the stability of the situation, not only in the region but also in the global international environment and vice versa. Our security has become an integral part of international security and stability. Membership in NATO is not only a project of the defence system of the Slovenian Army and the Ministry of Defence, but a project of the whole country. The involvement in NATO has particularly affected the long-term planning of defence reforms and the development of the Slovenian army in recent years. The goals we set ourselves are so realistic and realistic because we take into account our real possibilities, resources and knowledge. Such an approach has established us in the alliance and also in the world as reliable interlocutors and partners. This is all the more so because we are aware that the collective defence system is based on reciprocity. Which means that Slovenia has both rights and duties in this context. This is also confirmed by the fact that over a thousand members of the Slovenian Army have participated in various peace operations in recent years. More than 200 members of the Slovenian armed forces are currently operating in NATO, the European Union and the United Nations. At the beginning of this millennium, we have adopted the most important strategic, developmental, normative and other documents on which the implementation of defence reforms is based. Stable public and policy support and a sufficient material basis have enabled us to change the army's replenishment system and start implementing a new force structure. We have begun to change the entire system of military training and education. Despite some problems, the vast majority of members of the Slovenian Armed Forces support these changes and is their key vehicle. Membership in NATO is also a kind of validation of the effectiveness of their work as well as of our work together. Despite the big changes, we have not forgotten that the Slovenian Army must remain ours, Slovenian military traditions must live in its organisation and operation. Ladies and gentlemen, even at this moment of celebration, there is no time for sleeping on laurels. We've reached full membership in Nat. In the strategic defence review, we assessed last year's state of play in the implementation of defence reforms. We have set long-term guidelines for their future development, we are preparing a new general, long-term programme for the development and equipping of the Slovenian Armed Forces by 2015. It will specify the solutions and measures for the further development of units and forces that will be able to carry out collective defence tasks effectively. Dear Members and Members! Membership in NATO is a long-term process that will be a constant in the development of our defence system of the Slovenian Armed Forces and the whole country. Future objectives and plans are clear, realistic and, above all, achievable. They will be reached by the Slovenian Army and the Ministry of Defence. Thank you.

We move on to the first defensive proposal for a law on a supportive business environment. The draft law was submitted by the Government of the Republic of Slovenia for consideration by the assembly. With regard to this law, a group of 10 Members, signed first by Mr Anton Anderlic, requested that the national assembly hold a general debate. ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u35	Dear Members and Members! The substantive reasons for issuing the new law stem from the changed conditions of management and the challenge of global competition followed by the development policy of Slovenia. This is an increase in the competitive capacity of the economy to operate on the global market, creating a dynamic structure of the economy and increasing the share of high added value activities, which requires first and foremost exposure to an environment conducive to entrepreneurship and innovation. Therefore, the basic objective of the law is to create an entrepreneurial environment that activates human, financial and material frameworks for easy entry and successful business growth and to optimise the exploitation of entrepreneurial and innovation potentials in Slovenia and includes: raising entrepreneurial culture in the environment and establishing an effective support system for access to information and services for entrepreneurs, which will contribute to the accelerated creation and successful operation of small and medium-sized enterprises, medium-sized enterprises, the development of an innovation environment, forms of knowledge-and-economy linkages that will accelerate and increase the efficiency of knowledge transfer to products, services and processes, as they will contribute to the faster development and growth of new technology-intensive enterprises and the technological and developmental capacities of active companies. Improving the availability and access to financial resources, strengthening the financial market in Slovenia and stimulating private and other sources of finance for investment investments in the enterprise sector, and in particular for the provision of initial and risk capital, which will contribute to greater developmental orientation and faster growth of small and medium-sized enterprises and to accelerating the creation of new innovative companies. The proposed law consists of seven chapters regulating the object and purpose of the law and presenting with the European Union a harmonised definition of small and medium-sized enterprises and micro-enterprises. Legal basis for the adoption of a national programme to promote entrepreneurship. The law lays down the basis for directing State budget resources on the basis of a programme of measures to encourage entrepreneurship. The programme shall be adopted by the Government of the Republic of Slovenia for a period of five years or a period consistent with the financial perspective of the European Union. The importance of the programme is to set guidelines and priorities for policy implementation in relation to strategic documents and development trends, the basis for the planning and use of European Union funds, structural funds and other resources, and the basis for participation in Community programmes and international comparisons. Furthermore, it also defines the implementation of the tasks and programmes of the entrepreneurial environment. The State shall carry out tasks in the area of development of the business support environment through a public institution to promote entrepreneurship or a public agency to promote entrepreneurship and by conferring public authority. This is currently being done by the small-economy acceleration centre. The law defines the possibilities of linking a public institution with other public law institutions, provided that this makes it possible to perform tasks more efficiently and more effectively. Innovation and transfer of knowledge and new technologies. In this area, the new law lays down the legal basis for keeping records of the institutions of the innovative environment, rules for the allocation of public-interest services and for the functioning of institutions that contribute to the faster creation and transfer of knowledge and the strengthening of the innovation capacity of enterprises and provide an enabling environment for the creation and initial operation of new innovative companies. This will contribute to a more transparent and efficient channelling of public funds in support of the functioning of institutions, strengthening the capacity of institutions to deliver services in a high-quality manner, reducing administrative costs at national level, more direct and professional targeting of resources in relation to the development priorities of individual sectors or groups of enterprises. Building institutions of an innovative environment also provides an important basis for establishing a quality implementation structure for the use of the European Union's development resources. Furthermore, the law also introduces measures by the State to improve access to financing of development investments for small, micro, small and medium-sized enterprises implemented by the Slovenian Enterprise Fund. The Public Fund of the Republic of Slovenia for the Development of a Small Economy in Maribor is established under the Law on Public Funds and the Law on the Promotion of a Small Economy. During the period 1996 to 2002, on the basis of the earmarked capital, 10 billion tolars were achieved and, according to the legally defined possible instruments, significant results were achieved. The analysis produced shows that access to funding sources for SMEs is still the most vulnerable problem where measures aimed at the following are needed in particular. The development of new instruments that will provide support in all stages of development and in order of investment needed by companies for successful development. The instrument of lowering the interest rate, which was an overweighted instrument, the main instrument of the current fund for the development of small and medium-sized enterprises is losing importance. The need to provide guarantees for leased loans is highlighted. New companies, early growth of start-ups and capital investments in risk projects, in new, innovative start-ups and early growth stages. Connecting financial incentives to other forms of support for small and medium-sized enterprises in particular through the development of services that will increase the supply of quality development projects and improve accessibility to funding sources. Use of public funds to a greater extent to attract and increase the investment of commercial financial institutions in projects of small and medium-sized enterprises. This is about strengthening the indirect role of the financial institution and linking with banks, venture capital funds, international financial institutions and the like, and also increasing the volume of earmarked funds. It is because of these findings that the law supports guidelines and measures to improve access to funding sources for small and medium-sized enterprises by providing the basis for the development of new instruments and under the existing fund as a response to the provision of shortcomings as new needs. Before that, I would like to mention that, just last week, the Fund signed a contract with the European Investment Fund to obtain an important guarantee potential. This law gives the possibility that the entrepreneurship fund - as proposed in this law - will be able to obtain a legal basis for this fund to be renamed in Maribor, so that it can draw on and benefit from other financial instruments that the European Commission also offers and enables all members through this European Investment Fund. However, the latest developments in this law are also public powers to implement the Programme of Action. The law provides that legal persons of public and private law acting in the field of entrepreneurship may, for a certain period, be given public authority by a decision on the basis of a public tender to carry out the administrative tasks defined in the Programme of Action. In order to obtain a public authorisation, the legal person must fulfil the conditions laid down in the law. The draft law also provides for the adoption of a sub-legislative regulation that will give more precise effect to the conditions, criteria, criteria and procedure for granting public authority. This may be withdrawn from the holder of the public authority if irregularities have been identified in his business, if the tasks have not been carried out in accordance with the programme, if the tasks are not carried out in time and there are major irregularities in the performance of the tasks. Dear Members and Members, if you support the proposal for a law on a supportive environment for entrepreneurship, you will make an important contribution to the elimination of some of the key barriers to the development of small and medium-sized enterprises and entrepreneurship and, of course, contribute significantly to the creation of a dynamic and vital structure of the Slovenian economy. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u37	Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman. Dear colleagues and colleagues! In transition countries, in those who are on the transition from planning or more or less agreed economies where, as in Slovenia, old models of opinion still prevail, this remains unresolved. It's an innovation environment. In particular, the proposaler wants to improve the knowledge transfer system to micro, small and medium-sized enterprises, as well as the development of the innovation environment itself. And third time, finance. The proposer seeks to improve the availability of and access to financial resources through this law. The possibility is seen by the proposer mainly in strengthening the financial market in Slovenia, stimulating private and other sources of financing with the purpose of investing in the business sector. The solutions that the proposer seeks to achieve are, in particular, the following. For the first time, there is a clear segmentation of companies to micro, small and medium, with a particularly important focus on the micro segment, which means an enterprise with up to ten employees and up to 470 million annual realizations. Small businesses that employ up to 50 people and reach up to 2.3 billion annual transport and medium-sized enterprises that employ up to 250 people and reach up to 11 and a half billion annual transport. Furthermore, there is a group of solutions that lays down the programming bases for the promotion of entrepreneurship and competitiveness. Then, in an entrepreneurial environment, the law provides the possibility of connecting a public institution with other public law institutions. New solutions in the area of an innovative environment are in line with the Lisbon strategy, innovation policy and industrial policy. In the financial environment, solutions focus mainly on developing new financial instruments, linking financial incentives with other forms of support to micro, small and medium-sized enterprises and intensifying the investment of commercial financial institutions in micro, small and medium-sized enterprises. If we also assess the financial consequences of this law, I must first find that the law has relatively modest financial ambitions. It represents about 0.6 per cent of the annual budget. That's probably modest. Of course, there is a question to which it would be useful to answer below whether such a level of investment is appropriate, too small or too high. Of course, before we begin to actively express our ambitions to increase these investments, but it would probably be useful to strengthen the third chapter, a proper assessment of the financial implications, with a slightly clearer presentation of the dynamic relationship between inputs and effects, what investment so far has brought us, what is the relationship between the cost of operating the system and effective investment in micro, small and medium-sized enterprises. Otherwise, the LDS proposal is considered suitable for further consideration and will be supported.

ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u50	Thank you, colleague Kelemina. The position of the Parliamentary Group of the Party of Young Slovenia, Igor Štemberger has the floor. Please.
ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u51	Respectable. In recent years, as Slovenia's entry into the European Union became closer and closer, the concept of national interest, the competitiveness of Slovenian companies and the fear of hostile foreign takeovers was repeatedly highlighted. On many occasions, it has been asked in itself what will happen to smaller Slovenian companies at the time of entry: will they be able to adapt adequately to the European market, maintain an appropriate market share and thus maintain the company's associated growth and maintain jobs? Fear of taking over foreign companies has been further strengthened in recent years, with the case of the Pivovarna Union and not least with Tobacco in Ljubljana, where we have seen clearly that the objectives of foreign owners are mainly profits and much less economic and social development of Slovenia. In the Young Slovenia Party, we are convinced that the causes of fear should not be attributed only to the latter, as the government could do much more effectively in this area by promoting entrepreneurship, a supportive environment, development, knowledge, innovation, in short, to all known factors that directly affect the higher competitiveness of the economy. Also an appropriate tax policy, allowing easier access to initial capital, creating conditions and infrastructure for start-up companies are measures that the government has not provided to an appropriate extent, and therefore the situation in Slovenian entrepreneurship is as envious as it is. It is expected that entry itself will not have a significant impact on the business of large Slovenian companies, nor on the small and medium-sized sector, since companies that are export-oriented to the European Union market have in fact had to adapt long ago. However, the lack of awareness of the long-term effects of Slovenia's entry into the European Union on entrepreneurship will have a lasting impact. Candidate countries, including Slovenia, had to adapt certain mechanisms and instruments before entry to European legal order and business policy, but we all know that this is not enough. National support mechanisms are also needed, a development-oriented economic policy, which is yet to be detected in Slovenia. Therefore, the tasks to be performed by Slovenia before the final entry into the European Union are related to the design and adaptation of strategic documents in the field of the economy, the promotion of the internationalisation of enterprises and the restructuring of sectors with the greatest structural difficulties. It is illustrative to expect all Slovenian companies to be ready for the European market and new operating conditions. Some, especially in the labour-intensive sectors, will need the country's financial assistance in restructuring, and it is all the more important to create the conditions for the development of new technologically advanced enterprises by means of well-developed business incubators and support environments. By entering the European Union, Slovenia will be entitled to draw funds from the European Structural Funds, which are also intended to promote the development of entrepreneurship, to promote, link and speed up the transfer of knowledge into the economy and to develop new financial instruments. It is therefore the task of the Government to provide support to all those who need it, as well as to provide the necessary budgetary resources for the establishment of coherent regional development. Enterprise knowledge, which needs to be incorporated into the school system, new ideas, adequate infrastructure and capital, is a prerequisite for economic development and thus for companies successfully competing in the European market. According to the opinion of the Young Slovenia Party, the objectives of the bill are aimed at this direction and we will support the proposal at this stage, and in the second reading we also propose amendments, which we believe will make the law even better and, above all, more effective than the one submitted to the National Assembly by the Government. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u71	Your Honor! It will be almost a year after the National Assembly has announced a change to the Constitution and added 3a. Article, which was also the basis for the accession of the Republic of Slovenia to the European Union. This Article sets out the relationship between the Government and the National Assembly and must be adopted by the State Assembly by a two-thirds majority, in order, however, to ensure that it follows both the constitutional provisions and the strong will of the people in the referendum to transfer to the Government, by accession of the Republic of Slovenia to the European Union, the State Assembly in some way, to the Government, part of the functions that it has had under the current conditions itself. It is therefore part of legislative decision-making and it is that, through this way of action, the National Assembly, by amending the Constitution and by means of an accession treaty, has transferred part of its functions to the European Union body. The marriage was prepared for a long time. They were different opinions. Whether to take a position where we would firmly commit the government and thereby in some way make it impossible for the government to act more flexibly in a weekly, day-to-day manner and when it comes to decision-making in Brussels, to a second model, the former is conditionally called Danish, the latter Finnish, when the government and the Parliament are working together in a way that Parliament gives its opinion, forms its views that they are not so binding that, in the context of the needs that will arise daily, it would not be possible to change these views on its own and then, later, of course, when such an amended one was established, later explain them in the National Assembly. I believe that in this too this design of positions would be more flexible and more general, to say so, but not specifically on a particular subject. At the second-reading meeting of the Committee on Internal Policy, following the first hearing of the meeting of the assembly, where the law was given an appropriate majority, it could say quite a large majority, then the Committee amended some of the provisions. Especially when it comes to naming individual organs. The bodies have been replaced by the term "institutions", since both the European Council and the Council of the European Union are an institution where, in fact, the Government of the Republic of Slovenia - as well as all governments - adopts the country's views or represents and represents the Republic of Slovenia. We have also replaced the word association of the 'establishment treaties of the European Union', which is too general, with the term 'contracts on which the European Union is founded'. In order to ensure the representation of the protection of the interests of the Republic of Slovenia, it was also necessary to lay down procedures for informing the national assembly and procedures for establishing positions. We have thus added a new second paragraph, which obliges the National Assembly to take account of the European Council and the EU Council's timetable. In cases where, for certain reasons, the National Assembly does not define itself until the proposal for a government position, it is considered that it agrees with this proposal of a government position. For the sake of the greater public, we have also set out the government's obligation to report before the EU Council and at ministerial level before Slovenia enters the EU. The amended bill is therefore in line with the comments of the legislative and legal service. The comments of the Government and the European Affairs Commission in Parliament are taken into account to a greater extent. I believe that it is perfectly well prepared with regard to the discussions in the first reading, so I propose that we also confirm this by accepting it.

ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u73	Thank you so much for saying that. Ladies and gentlemen of the government, the House of Respect! At its 47th regular meeting on 12.02.2004, the Committee of the National Assembly of the Republic of Slovenia considered the draft law on cooperation between the national assembly and the government in matters of the European Union submitted to the national assembly by the group of Members with the first signed by Anton Anderlic. The amendments to the bill were tabled by the Government of the Republic of Slovenia. It was presented by the Slovenian National Party's Parliamentary Group and the European Affairs Commission as an interested working body. The debate took place in a way typical of regulating such relations. The National Assembly wanted as much government oversight as possible, and the government tried to get out of that embrace. Thus, the Government's representative came from the active role of the Government in enforcing the positions of the Republic of Slovenia in the European Union and from its constitutional and legal position and operation. In particular, he stressed the importance of establishing a mechanism for cases where the national assembly, for certain reasons, does not define a proposal for a government position. He further committed himself to the solution that the meetings of the working bodies of the national assembly would be closed to the public because of the specific nature of the European Union, and also proposed that the National Assembly should not take any views on the amendments to the Treaties on which the Union is founded, but should only discuss them. The Council of State would also like its piece of cooperation, and the representative stressed that the Council of State has a clear and undisputed limit of its powers, a certain impact on the legislative functioning of the National Assembly. He highlighted in particular Article 6 of the bill that the competent working bodies of the national assembly also take into account the opinions of the State Council or its working bodies when dealing with European matters. In doing so, the members of the Committee felt that the position of the Council of State with its entry into the European Union was not changing and that it would be able to continue to give opinions on all matters to be dealt with by the National Assembly. The President of the European Affairs Commission stressed that the content or timing of the legislative process in the institutions of the European Union will sometimes not require or time to deal with the matter first, and then the labour force responsible for European affairs. In this case, he proposed supplementing Article 6 of the draft law by dealing with European work force and the working body responsible for foreign policy, as a rule, with European matters on the basis of the opinions of the working bodies. In the ensuing debate, the members of the Committee paid particular attention to the regulation of the relationship between the National Assembly and the Government with regard to the adoption of legal acts and decisions within the European Union and stressed the important appropriate regulation of the internal aspect of this external relationship by holding the Government responsible for the State's policy and for the decisions taken in the European Union. Therefore, as a key issue, they highlighted the arrangements for the role and position of the National Assembly vis-à-vis the Government as an equivalent body in shaping the positions of the Republic of Slovenia on those European matters which would otherwise fall within its competence. In doing so, the government should make a choice of matters and also take political responsibility for them. It is important, however, that the Government, in view of its assessment of the political importance of certain European matters, may also propose its treatment for the purpose of obtaining the views of the National Assembly to the proposal for the views of the Republic of Slovenia. The members of the Committee did not agree with the opinion of the Government, which was summarised in the amendment to Article 2, and highlighted in particular the requirement to maintain the role of the parent labour bodies in relation to both labour bodies responsible for European affairs and foreign affairs. The Committee adopted amendments to Article 8 tabled by the European Affairs Commission and then amendments [[...]] [[A warning sign for the end of the debate.]] Article 5 has not been adopted by some amendments to the European Affairs Commission, the Governments of the Republic of Slovenia and the Slovenian National Party's Parliamentary Group, and some amendments have become irrelevant. Thank you very much.
