ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u162	Mr. Vice President, thank you very much for your words. Dear colleagues and colleagues! The proposed law implementing the customs rules of the European Community provides for a more detailed regulation of the provisions of the customs code and other customs rules of the Community, which empowers Member States to continue to regulate the area in national legislation. Some provisions, however, where the European Customs Code refers to regulation in the laws of the Member States, govern areas not regulated by Community customs legislation. No transitional periods or exceptions have been negotiated in the process of negotiations for accession to the European Union in the section on customs union as regards compliance with European Community legislation. For this reason, the operation of our economic operators with economic operators from other Member States of the European Union will also be based on the freedoms of the internal European market, and trade in goods from third countries will continue to be subject to customs rules. The draft law further regulates the individual provisions of the Community legislation which have its basis for European customs policy in the customs law, which contains the basic provisions on how legal and natural persons must deal with goods imported from or exported to third countries by a Community territory of third countries and who, when and in what way must pay import or export customs duties. Our 1995 customs law in force, which was later supplemented by some of the amendments to the law, and on the basis of it, the subcontracting acts issued are almost fully in line with the customs law as a basic act and other customs rules of the Community. However, those particular areas which could not be taken over before membership will also cease to apply from the date of membership of the Slovenian customs rules. The drafter also decided, following the example of some members of the community, to regulate certain areas where the country felt the need for national regulation by adopting a specific law. The Legislative and Legal Service of the National Assembly has made certain comments to the draft law, to individual articles, which the Committee has mostly taken into account in the drafting and adoption of its amendments, as set out in the accompanying report of the Committee. I would also like to touch on the discussions on the Committee, where particular attention has been paid to the provision of Article 109 relating to the arrangements for the conversion of free customs shops into border stores or transitional arrangements for non-Community goods. It has already been mentioned that the parent committee has not adopted its amendment to Article 109 of the Act. In the Group of Members of the United List of Social Democrats, we would like to point out that the above-mentioned amendment of the Section of Free Customs Shops in the Association for Tourism and the Restaurant of the Chamber of Commerce of Slovenia requires a re- or re-examination of the amendment from all aspects, as the transformation of free-carrying shops into border trade does not provide employment for employees now in the premises of customs shops at the Slovenian-Croatian border and will result in indirect loss of more jobs in distributors. ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u164	Thank you very much for your words, Mr. Vice President. Government representatives, colleagues and colleagues! We have before us a proposal for a law implementing the customs rules of the European Community. It also states: This law regulates the implementation of the customs rules of the European Community, hereinafter referred to as ‘the Community’, which empowers Member States to continue to regulate the field of customs rules in national legislation and regulates matters in this field that are not within the competence of the Community. The position of our parliamentary group, the Slovenian People's Party, on the bill is as follows. At the time of entry into the European Union, the Republic of Slovenia will fully apply the European rules governing the functioning of the customs union, since no transitional periods or exceptions have been negotiated in the process of accession to the section on customs union, Chapter 25. Given that customs Union regulations are regulated in Council Regulations and Commission Regulations and Conventions or Conventions directly applicable, their transposition into national legislation will not be necessary. Although a large majority of the powers in the implementation of customs policy have been transferred to the European institutions, there remain some areas where Member States have the option of national regulation. In doing so, it was decided to regulate such areas in a special law, in the so-called national customs law. The proposed law aims to regulate in more detail the provisions of the Customs Code and other customs rules of the Community which empower Member States to continue to regulate the area in national legislation, or provisions where the European Customs Code refers to regulation in the laws of the Member States and areas not regulated by Community customs legislation. In our parliamentary group, we believe that the concept of resolution in the draft law of those areas is good and useful for Slovenia. At the same time, we estimate that the proposal for a law has also taken on both substantively good and optimally all these areas. On the basis of this, we assess that the proposed law with amendments to the Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy is appropriate and necessary for adoption and that it represents a good legislative solution in this area, and that we will therefore support the Members of our SLS Group. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u186	Thank you very much for your words, Madam Vice President. Dear Members and Members! You have before you, as you have heard, a proposal for a law amending and supplementing the Defence Act, which is actually a second phase of complementing the system's legislation in the field of national defence, which we have also announced in 2002. What are the objectives or objectives of our proposed changes? Firstly, complement the provisions, namely those related to our membership and operation in the collective defence system, as have been agreed with our NATO experts and experts in the last year. Secondly, to harmonise or improve certain provisions in the light of the experience gained in the enforcement of the system legislation applicable to the public sector in order to allow for the consideration of the characteristics of defence work, particularly in the military field. Thirdly, legal solutions that will allow for the start of the elimination of the human resources imbalances that were not covered by the amendments to the law in 2002. Fourthly, to allow for the continuation of defence reforms, in view of the findings in the 2002/2003 strategic review, which we sent to the National Security Council for a preliminary hearing, and in which the vision for the development of the Slovenian army is also covered by 2015. In view of these objectives, as the most important innovations in the draft law, I underline the following solutions. The control and defence of the airspace will be provided by the Slovenian military within the integrated air defence system of the North Atlantic Alliance. This also applies to defence, including the airspace of certain vital facilities which may be relevant to the defence of the State under certain conditions. If necessary, the Government will arrange procedures for the implementation of airspace control. We will include in this system our own radar and other military assets that we have at our disposal. In the framework of the Alliance, however, the required resources will be provided which we do not have. The Ministry of Defence will be the competent national authority in the field of security of classified data in information and telecommunications systems for defence purposes, cryptic protection, for protection against unwanted electromagnetic radiation and for carrying out radio spectrum for defence purposes. The powers of the military police will be aligned with those of the military police of other military organisations we meet in peacekeeping operations. For the same reasons, we also introduce military security authorities into the law, even though they are part of the internal military structure and formation. The mandate of the military is to extend the law so that it can help the police in an appropriate way to protect the state border, deep down, without police powers. This is a solution that has its basis between measures for effective entry into the European Union and which has been examined in the Interdepartmental Working Group and is also known by some comparable Member States of the European Union. The law sets out in more detail the solutions for the appointment of military personnel to formational duties, including the highest in the military, the positions of defence inspectors, the official evaluation of military personnel, and the conduct of military service in parts outside the military, such as military attachés and the like. It is mainly about complementing existing solutions in order to make these even clearer and even more comprehensive. With the aim of putting an end to the staffing imbalances in the permanent composition of the Slovenian army and preventing their further expansion, we propose a system-wide solution to end the working relationship of a military person in the defence area once he fulfils the conditions for obtaining the right to an old pension. Once the law is in force, the working relationship will end for some 60 people, a different solution, if we take into account the recognition of the bleached working age in the past or now additional insurance, probably not justified. There are about 200 disabled persons in the military and in the Ministry and we propose a one-time measure to apply the existing 404th Article of the Pension Insurance Act to women over 50 years of age and 47 years of age, so that, under the conditions laid down by the law, a maximum of 77 disabled persons who cannot be provided with adequate work in the Ministry and even less in the military can be retired. The costs of such a measure will be borne by defence expenditure. The proposed solution would reduce the number of people with disabilities, which would facilitate the resolution of their situation in the Ministry, especially if we take into account the solution in the system's part of the law, where we propose that disabled persons in the military can be temporarily deployed outside the formation of units while they are being sought for adequate work in relation to the remaining working capacity. For all other discrepancies arising in the permanent composition of the army due to age, inadequate personnel structures, given the size of the army, we estimate that they can be resolved in the medium to long term through other measures. This means that we are proposing significantly more rational solutions than those in 2001. This is also evident from the summary of the solutions in the introduction of the law used by other armies to address staff surpluses and non-conformities. Solutions relating to the acquisition of missing education, omissions from actions, as well as the temporary allocation to formational duties derogating from the system regime are the result of the experience gained in the past period in the implementation of system-wide public sector legislation and in the introduction of a new organisation and structure of the Slovenian Armed Forces. The problem of lack of education in employees is solved in the draft law as it has been solved for other public servants. The introduction of fines instead of fines and certain other transitional provisions only means coordination with other existing rules. Dear Members and Members! We estimate that the carefully proposed amendments and amendments to the law are adopted before full membership in NATO, as this will facilitate our operation after June 2004. On the other hand, solutions that could hinder the further implementation of defence reforms will be exceeded. The proposal for legislative amendments is in line with the relevant union in the work on status issues. I therefore propose, ladies and gentlemen, that you support and adopt the proposal of the law. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u188	Thank you, Madam Vice President. Welcome government representatives, colleagues and colleagues. The proposed Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Defence Act complements the existing law in particular with provisions on the employment, promotion and retirement of military personnel. It is part of a set of regulations which are necessary for the reorganization and completion of the Slovenian Armed Forces and the membership of Slovenia in NATO. The bill covers three sets. The first contains provisions allowing for cooperation with NATO. It refers to the protection of confidential data, the coordination of spatial acts and material duties. The second unit refers to the new organisation of the army. Its essence is to reduce the military composition and to allocate different powers between the General Staff and the operational level, where the role of the General Staff is strengthened. This provides that only professional soldiers may be assigned in peace to carry out their duties outside the country, in war or in the use of military forces in the collective defence system, as well as members of the military system. In peace, military reserves may also be sent to training outside the country. The system of promotion and the supplementary provisions on military discipline have also been reorganized. The third set deals with matters relating to the profession in the defence field and the conduct of military service. Military police. This may investigate offences in the military for which a fine or imprisonment is prescribed for up to three years. The Slovenian army does not have its own military judiciary. There is nothing explicit about health care in the article on material and health care. We also have private health care and private health workers in our country. If necessary, these should also be included in defence or protection. ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u208	Dear colleagues and colleagues! Representatives or government representatives! The Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia stipulates that parents have the right and duty to maintain, educate and raise their children. In particular, maintenance, although not limited to it, also stresses the obligation to invest in the benefit of children of material resources. Since the burden of maintaining children is left to society and even imposes the burden on parents, it is also their responsibility to provide them with the necessary care and assistance. Accordingly, the Constitution provides that the State shall protect the family, motherhood, fatherhood, children and youth and shall create the necessary conditions. Article 53 of the Constitution provides for this. The State provides for conditions that enable parents to decide on the birth of their children, as is true in the Constitution, and that children enjoy special protection and care. It is therefore incompatible with those constitutional provisions and the principle of the social state that a parent who, from that part of the funds intended for the survival of children or the minimum of living, contributes at the same time to social needs. Therefore, the necessary maintenance costs must be fully exempt from taxation. That is also the case with the Constitutional Court, which has been pronounced with regard to the provisions of the Income Act. It is high time that the unconstitutional status of the Income Act is abolished, that this unconstitutional state of the Income Act is immediately eliminated. So far, there has been no need for understanding to harmonise this constitutional provision, which is awaiting arrangements for a long seven years, which is certainly a disgrace to the legal system, to the National Assembly, and in particular to the Government, which has postponed the proposal for a Constitutional Court decision and a constitutional provision for a series of years. We have therefore given the initiative - a group of signatories - and have presented solutions that respect the constitutional provision, the resolution on family policy in the Republic of Slovenia, the principle of solidarity and the principle of progressive relief for families with multiple members. The government and the coalition are clearly blind to the plight of families who have felt the unconstitutional state on their skin and have worn it for many years. The ruling coalition is very different when it comes to implementing constitutional provisions. As far as our children and citizens are concerned, they postpone it when it comes to foreign citizens, trying to establish a priority, forcefully and contrary to the rules of procedure or constitution. The proposed law, prepared by the signatories and fully supported in New Slovenia, which we have also tabled two years ago, exempts from taxation that part of the income of individual citizens and citizens with children, which means the most necessary means of child survival, i.e. to the extent of the minimum cost of living or the existential minimum for children. This is essentially the decision of the Constitutional Court. This decision is also a fundamental issue of justice in society.If we have decided on the principle of solidarity and reciprocity, then it is necessary for those who have children or more children to recognise the additional benefits and evaluate their contribution to the survival, upbringing and training of citizens, who eventually become taxpayers, operators of public interest and more. This is the basic civilisation principle of modern society. In New Slovenia, we have made it clear that there is a need to harmonise an unconstitutional law, but not in a way that reduces benefits for many families, but that this status must at least be maintained. This is the true principle of justice that we want to follow in New Slovenia. In Slovenia we should particularly support such solutions because we are in demographic decline. Only 17,500 children were regulated in Slovenia in the last year. The Constitutional Court does not resort to progressive relief. This is permissible in the light of the policy that each political party has on this issue. We are in favour of it, so we have also tabled our own law and presented a scale comparable to other systems and whose objectives are consistent with family and social policy. And that's exactly what the Constitutional Court's decision is about. Statistical data from European countries clearly show a positive correlation between the level of development of family policy and the rate of birth, with the majority of these countries implementing an important part of family policy also through tax policy, in addition to social, housing and others. Tax reliefs play an important role in this regard and work in one direction or another. The decision for the family or its extension shall be facilitated or made more difficult. It is also important to realize that with the number of children, income per family member falls. The progressiveness of tax relief, and this is what this is about, ensures that children are taken into account as a factor in determining the economic situation of the debtor and the related principle of horizontal equality, which requires that the debtors pay approximately the same high taxes in a similar economic situation. However, with sufficient progressive tax reliefs for children, it is ensured that those in comparable economic situations - comparable professions, employment, wages - do not lower the standard of living so strongly as to make it an important factor or obstacle to decision-making for children. We thus understand the decision of the Constitutional Court. The progressive tax rate of non-taxable minimum child living costs ensures the coordinated functioning of tax benefits for families with higher incomes and a child allowance for families with lower incomes. This means that the combined impact of both factors in all families with the same number of children is the same, irrespective of the income of these families. And it is precisely because of the dependency on income that tax reliefs operate that is significantly more stimulating than direct transfers. We're pretty sure that's the way it is. The positive dependence on income relief is an additional incentive for individual efforts, while usually the negative link between income income and income income, for example, lower income, more from the budget, good incentives for concealing income or professional efforts for greater success, and this, in our opinion, certainly does not encourage. In our view, there is still a real possibility that, in the context of amendments to the Income Law, the legislator will consider the concept of family income, which means that families with equally high common incomes will pay roughly the same high income regardless of the structure of these incomes. Today, in the same incomes, families in which only one parent earns all income pay substantially more income than the family in which each parent earns half of that income. This seems unfair in terms of justice. Family income, however, would ensure that families in which one parent chooses to work shorter hours would not pay more income than the families in which both parents are employed. Colleagues, when proposing this Income Act, of course, did not think about all these elements. This is a highly complex matter and undoubtedly requires some substantial changes in the very concept of this law. But the Slovenian People's Party's Parliamentary Group will continue to support the stimulating taxation of the income of natural persons, either in this law or most likely in the government law coming into the procedure in the coming months, and here we are devoting special treatment to families with the arguments I have just stated. And we estimate that the National Assembly will support a solution towards progressive relief for the maintenance of a family member. Thank you very much.

We will support this amendment on our own and within the Group of Members. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2004-02-24-SDZ3-Redna-34.ana.u273	Thank you very much. When we prepared and adopted the first Rules of Procedure of the new National Assembly 12 years ago, I was chairman of the Rules of Procedure Commission. We had an interview - some of you colleagues were - we also had an interview with representatives, experts from the European Community, who warned us that we would be one of the few countries that would have working bodies open to the public. When we adopted this Rules of Procedure, this issue was again raised and we decided together not to change it because it has become a practice in Slovenia, although this is not a practice in Europe. To talk about not being public, but not being true. Following the decisions taken, the public will be fully informed of what we have decided. However, tactical issues of alignment with European countries may not make sense that they are public. That's why I don't support such a solution. Thank you.
